Image name: PG_task3_gt2_PAIR1
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 11
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are N (black) and G (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect N from G.

1.  **Identify direct connections to target nodes:**
    *   Node G is connected to nodes X, K, and H.
    *   Node N is connected to nodes Y, O, and B.

2.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   If we remove all edges directly connected to G, G will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from N. The edges connected to G are (G, X), (G, K), and (G, H). Removing these 3 edges would disconnect G. This gives a cut set of size 3.
    *   Similarly, if we remove all edges directly connected to N, N will be completely isolated. The edges connected to N are (N, Y), (N, O), and (N, B). Removing these 3 edges would disconnect N. This also gives a cut set of size 3.

3.  **Check for a smaller cut (size 1 or 2):**
    *   **Cut of size 1:** A single edge removal would disconnect N from G only if that edge is a bridge that all paths between N and G must traverse.
        *   Node G has a degree of 3. Removing any single edge connected to G (e.g., (G, X)) would still leave paths to G via (G, K) or (G, H).
        *   Node N has a degree of 3. Removing any single edge connected to N (e.g., (N, Y)) would still leave paths from N via (N, O) or (N, B).
        *   Therefore, no single edge can disconnect N from G.

    *   **Cut of size 2:** We need to determine if there are two edges whose removal would disconnect N from G.
        *   **Consider edges directly connected to G:** If we remove any two edges connected to G (e.g., (G, X) and (G, K)), the third edge (G, H) would still provide a path to G. For example, N - B - C - H - G would still be a valid path. Thus, removing two edges directly connected to G is not sufficient.
        *   **Consider "bottleneck" edges further from G:** All paths from N to G must eventually pass through one of the nodes H, K, or X, and then to G. Let's identify the edges that connect to the set {H, K, X} from the rest of the graph (excluding G itself). These edges are:
            *   (I, H)
            *   (C, H)
            *   (F, K)
            *   (F, X)
            (Note: (X, K) connects two nodes within the {H, K, X} set, so it's not an "entry" edge from the N-side.)
            There are 4 such edges. If we remove any two of these 4 edges, there will still be paths remaining:
            *   If we remove (I, H) and (C, H), paths via (F, K) and (F, X) are still open (e.g., N - B - E - F - K - G).
            *   If we remove (F, K) and (F, X), paths via (I, H) and (C, H) are still open (e.g., N - B - C - H - G).
            *   If we remove (I, H) and (F, K), paths via (C, H) and (F, X) are still open (e.g., N - B - C - H - G and N - B - E - F - X - G).
            In all combinations of removing two of these four edges, at least one path from N to G remains.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    Since a cut of size 3 has been identified (by isolating G or N), and it has been systematically shown that no cut of size 1 or 2 is sufficient to disconnect N from G, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, X), (G, K), (G, H)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['F', 'E'], ['G', 'H']]}

Image name: Tu_task2_gt2_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 11
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are L and J.
2.  **Identify all direct paths between L and J:**
    *   Path 1: L - M - J. This is a direct path through node M.
3.  **Identify all indirect paths between L and J:**
    *   Path 2: L - E - P - J.
    *   Path 3: L - E - M - J. (Note: This path also uses M, so if M is removed, this path is also broken).

4.  **Analyze the necessity of removing M:**
    The path L - M - J is a direct connection between L and J through a single intermediate node M. To break this path, node M must be removed, as it is the only non-target node on this specific path. Since M is not a black node, it can be removed. Therefore, M must be included in any cut set that disconnects L and J.

5.  **Consider the graph after removing M:**
    If M is removed, L is now only connected to E. J is now only connected to P and D.
    The path L - M - J is broken.
    The path L - E - M - J is also broken.

6.  **Identify remaining paths after M is removed:**
    With M removed, the only remaining path from L to J is L - E - P - J.

7.  **Determine additional nodes to remove:**
    To break the remaining path L - E - P - J, we must remove either node E or node P. Both E and P are non-target nodes and can be removed.

8.  **Formulate the minimum cut set:**
    Based on the analysis, the cut set must include M, and either E or P. This leads to two possible minimum cut sets:
    *   [M, E]
    *   [M, P]

    Both of these cut sets have a size of 2.

9.  **Verify:**
    *   If [M, E] are removed: L is disconnected from M and E, thus L has no paths to J.
    *   If [M, P] are removed: L is disconnected from M. The path L-E-P-J is broken because P is removed. Thus, L has no paths to J.

Since a single node removal (M, E, P, or D) is not sufficient to disconnect L and J (as shown in the thought process), and we found a cut set of size 2, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [M, E] or [M, P]

Image name: POGBb_task2_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 11
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect the two black nodes, E and I, I will first identify all possible paths between them. I am looking for vertex-disjoint paths (excluding the source E and sink I) to apply Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of vertices to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

I have identified the following three vertex-disjoint paths between E and I:

1.  **Path 1:** E - M - J - P - I
    *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J, P}
2.  **Path 2:** E - B - N - C - I
    *   Intermediate nodes: {B, N, C}
3.  **Path 3:** E - F - K - G - H - I
    *   Intermediate nodes: {F, K, G, H}

Let's verify that these paths are indeed vertex-disjoint (excluding E and I):
*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 1 is {M, J, P}.
*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {B, N, C}.
*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 3 is {F, K, G, H}.

These three sets of intermediate nodes are mutually disjoint. Since there are three vertex-disjoint paths, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect E and I.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3.
Consider the nodes directly connected to I: P, C, and H.
If I remove these three nodes:
*   Removing P breaks Path 1 (E - M - J - P - I) and any other path that might use P to reach I (e.g., E - M - J - D - P - I).
*   Removing C breaks Path 2 (E - B - N - C - I) and any other path that might use C to reach I (e.g., paths through O or Y to C).
*   Removing H breaks Path 3 (E - F - K - G - H - I) and any other path that might use H to reach I (e.g., E - B - H - I, if it existed, or paths from G to H).

By removing P, C, and H, node I becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, as these are its only direct neighbors. Therefore, no path can exist between E and I.

Since we established that a minimum of 3 nodes are required, and we found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [P, C, H]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': ['F', 'B', 'M']}
Progress: 7/280 images processed.

Image name: PG_task2_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 11
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are H and E. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect H from E. I cannot remove H or E.

2.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** The minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes (H and E) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them. I will systematically search for vertex-disjoint paths.

3.  **Find vertex-disjoint paths from H to E:**
    *   **Path 1:** H - I - J - M - L - E
        *   Intermediate nodes used: {I, J, M, L}

    *   **Path 2:** Now, I look for a path that does not use any of the nodes from Path 1's intermediate set ({I, J, M, L}).
        *   H - C - B - E
        *   Intermediate nodes used: {C, B}
        *   This set {C, B} is disjoint from {I, J, M, L}.

    *   **Path 3:** Now, I look for a path that does not use any of the nodes from Path 1 or Path 2's intermediate sets ({I, J, M, L, C, B}).
        *   H - X - K - F - E
        *   Intermediate nodes used: {X, K, F}
        *   This set {X, K, F} is disjoint from {I, J, M, L} and {C, B}.

4.  **Check for additional vertex-disjoint paths:**
    *   From H, the remaining unvisited neighbor is G.
    *   If I try to form a path starting H - G:
        *   H - G - F - E: Node F is already used in Path 3.
        *   H - G - K - F - E: Nodes K and F are already used in Path 3.
        *   H - G - X - K - F - E: Nodes X, K, F are already used in Path 3.
    *   Therefore, no new vertex-disjoint path can be formed starting with H - G.
    *   All other nodes (D, P, O, Y, N) are connected to the graph via nodes already used in Path 1 (I, J) or Path 2 (C). For example, any path through O would involve C or I, which are already used.

5.  **Conclusion on maximum vertex-disjoint paths:** I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

6.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**
    To disconnect E from H, I can remove all nodes directly connected to E (excluding H, which is not directly connected to E). The neighbors of E are B, F, and L.
    *   Removing B will cut all paths to E that go through B.
    *   Removing F will cut all paths to E that go through F.
    *   Removing L will cut all paths to E that go through L.
    Since B, F, and L are the only neighbors of E, removing them will completely isolate E from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from H.

7.  **Verify the cut set:** The set {B, F, L} consists of 3 nodes. Each node is a white circle, so they are not the target nodes H or E. Removing these three nodes indeed breaks all paths from H to E. Since the minimum number of nodes is 3, this is a minimal cut set.

The final answer is:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, F, L]

    *   Node J is directly connected to M, I, P, and D.

    A straightforward approach to disconnect two nodes is to isolate one of them by removing all its incident edges.
    *   To isolate L, I would need to remove the edges (L, E) and (L, M). This would require removing 2 edges.
    *   To isolate J, I would need to remove the edges (J, M), (J, I), (J, P), and (J, D). This would require removing 4 edges.
    Since 2 is less than 4, isolating L is a more promising candidate for a minimal cut.

3.  **Test for a cut set of size 1:**
    *   If I remove only (L, E), L is still connected to M, and M is connected to J (L-M-J). So, a path still exists.
    *   If I remove only (L, M), L is still connected to E. E is connected to I, and I is connected to J (L-E-I-J). So, a path still exists.
    Since removing any single edge does not disconnect L from J, the minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

4.  **Verify the cut set of size 2: [(L, E), (L, M)]**
    If the edges (L, E) and (L, M) are removed, node L becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Since L has no connections, no path can originate from L and reach J (or any other node). Therefore, L and J are completely disconnected.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    A cut set of size 2, specifically [(L, E), (L, M)], successfully disconnects L from J. Since a cut set of size 1 is not possible, 2 is the minimum number of edges required.

    *   **Path C:** E - L - M - J - P - I - H (Intermediate nodes: L, M, J, P, I)

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Since there are at least 3 node-disjoint paths between E and H, Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect E and H (the minimum vertex cut) must be at least 3.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**

3.  **Systematic Path Analysis:**

    *   **Paths from J to F can be broadly divided into two main groups based on how they reach F's immediate neighbors (E, K, X):**
        *   **Group 1: Paths that pass through node E to reach F.**
            *   J -> M -> E -> F
            *   J -> M -> L -> E -> F
            *   J -> P -> O -> Y -> N -> B -> E -> F
            *   J -> P -> I -> C -> N -> B -> E -> F
            *   J -> P -> I -> H -> B -> E -> F
            *   (And paths starting with J -> D -> P... which then follow the P paths)
            All paths in this group are broken if node **E** is removed.

        *   **Group 2: Paths that pass through nodes K or X to reach F (and thus do NOT use E).**
            *   To reach K or X, all paths must first pass through node G (since K and X are only connected to G on the "left" side, besides each other and F).
            *   To reach G, all paths from J must pass through node H (G is connected to H, K, X. H is connected to B and I. B is connected to N, C, H, E. I is connected to P, C, H).
            *   Let's list some examples of these paths:
                *   J -> P -> O -> Y -> N -> B -> H -> G -> K -> F
                *   J -> P -> O -> Y -> N -> B -> H -> G -> K -> X -> F
                *   J -> P -> I -> C -> N -> B -> H -> G -> K -> F
                *   J -> P -> I -> C -> N -> B -> H -> G -> K -> X -> F
                *   J -> P -> I -> H -> G -> K -> F
                *   J -> P -> I -> H -> G -> K -> X -> F
            All paths in this group are broken if node **H** is removed (because H is a common predecessor to G for all these paths).

4.  **Identify Minimum Cut Set:**
    Based on the analysis:
    *   Removing node **E** breaks all paths in Group 1.
    *   Removing node **H** breaks all paths in Group 2.

    Therefore, removing both **E** and **H** will disconnect J from F. This forms a cut set of size 2.

5.  **Verification:**
    *   If E is removed: All paths through E to F are severed.
    *   If H is removed: All paths that would otherwise go through H (e.g., J-...-H-G-K-F or J-...-H-G-K-X-F) are severed.
    *   No path remains from J to F.
    *   Since we established that a single node removal is not sufficient, a cut set of size 2 is the minimum.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F (black) and D (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect F from D.

1.  **Identify direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node F is connected to X, K, G, E.
    *   Node D is connected to J, P.

2.  **Analyze paths to D:**
    Any path from F to D must eventually pass through one of D's direct neighbors, J or P, and then use the edge connecting that neighbor to D. Therefore, all paths to D must use either the edge (J, D) or the edge (P, D).

3.  **Consider a cut set of size 1:**
    If I remove only one edge, for example (J, D), then paths through P (e.g., F-E-B-C-I-P-D) would still exist. If I remove only (P, D), paths through J (e.g., F-K-J-D) would still exist. Thus, a single edge removal is not sufficient to disconnect F and D. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

4.  **Consider a cut set of size 2:**
    Based on the observation that all paths to D must use either (J, D) or (P, D), removing both of these edges would completely isolate D from the rest of the graph.
    *   If (J, D) is removed, no path can reach D via node J.
    *   If (P, D) is removed, no path can reach D via node P.
    Since D has no other connections, removing both (J, D) and (P, D) ensures that no path exists between F and D. This is a cut set of size 2: [(J, D), (P, D)].

5.  **Verify minimality using Menger's Theorem (optional but good for confidence):**
    Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes. Let's find edge-disjoint paths from F to D:
    *   **Path 1:** F - K - J - D
        Edges: {(F, K), (K, J), (J, D)}
    *   **Path 2:** F - E - B - C - I - P - D
        Edges: {(F, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, I), (I, P), (P, D)}
    These two paths are completely edge-disjoint (they do not share any common edges).
    Can we find a third edge-disjoint path? Any path to D must use either (J, D) or (P, D). Both of these edges are already used by Path 1 and Path 2 respectively. Therefore, no third edge-disjoint path exists.
    Since there are exactly 2 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

6.  **Confirm no other cut set of size 2 works (as demonstrated in thought process):**
    I considered alternative cut sets of size 2 that do not include (J, D) or (P, D), such as [(K, J), (I, P)] or [(I, J), (O, P)]. In both cases, I found remaining paths between F and D, confirming that these sets are not valid cuts. This reinforces that [(J, D), (P, D)] is indeed a minimal cut.

The cut set [(J, D), (P, D)] is sufficient and minimal.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(J, D), (P, D)]

Let's analyze the "bottlenecks" or "choke points" in the graph. I'll consider a conceptual partition of the graph into two sets: one containing F (the "F-side") and one containing J (the "J-side"). The minimum number of edges to remove will be the minimum number of edges that cross such a partition.

Let's define a partition (S, T) where F is in S and J is in T.
Consider the set S = {F, K, X, G, E, L, M}. This set includes F and all nodes that are "close" to F and can lead to J via M, or via G, or via E.
The remaining nodes form set T = {J, P, I, D, H, B, C, O, Y, N}.

Now, I identify all edges that connect a node in S to a node in T. These are the edges that cross the cut (S, T):
1.  **(G, H)**: Node G is in S, Node H is in T. This edge connects the G-branch from F to the H-branch in T.
2.  **(E, B)**: Node E is in S, Node B is in T. This edge connects the E-branch from F to the B-branch in T.
3.  **(M, J)**: Node M is in S, Node J is in T. This edge connects the M-branch from F (via E or L) directly to J.

The set of edges crossing this partition is {(G, H), (E, B), (M, J)}. This set has 3 edges.

Now, I need to verify that removing these three edges indeed disconnects F from J, and that this is the minimum number of edges.

**Verification of disconnection:**
*   **Paths through M**: Any path from F to J that goes through M (e.g., F-E-M-J, F-E-L-M-J) will be cut by the removal of edge (M, J).
*   **Paths through E (not via M)**: Any path from F to J that goes through E and then B (e.g., F-E-B-H-I-J, F-E-B-C-I-J, F-E-B-C-O-P-J, F-E-B-N-O-P-J) will be cut by the removal of edge (E, B). Since M is handled by (M,J), and L only connects to E and M, all paths from F through E are now blocked.
*   **Paths through G (and K, X)**: Any path from F to J that goes through G (e.g., F-G-H-I-J, F-G-H-B-C-I-J, F-G-H-B-C-O-P-J, F-G-H-B-N-O-P-J) will be cut by the removal of edge (G, H). Paths from F through K or X eventually lead to G (e.g., F-K-G-H-..., F-X-G-H-...), so these are also cut by (G, H).

Since all possible routes from F to J are covered by the removal of these three edges, F and J will be completely disconnected.

**Verification of minimality:**
To prove that 3 is the minimum, I must show that no set of 2 edges can disconnect F and J.
Let's consider removing any two edges from the proposed cut set:
1.  If I remove (G, H) and (E, B): A path F-E-M-J still exists.
2.  If I remove (G, H) and (M, J): A path F-E-B-H-I-J (or F-E-B-C-I-J, etc.) still exists.
3.  If I remove (E, B) and (M, J): A path F-G-H-I-J (or F-G-H-B-C-I-J, etc.) still exists.

Since removing any two of these edges leaves at least one path between F and J, a minimum of 3 edges is required.

    *   **Path 2:** H - G - F - M - L - E - Y - N (Intermediate nodes: G, F, M, L, E, Y)
    *   **Path 3:** H - I - C - N (Intermediate nodes: I, C)

    Let's check if these paths are truly vertex-disjoint (excluding H and N):
    *   Nodes in Path 1 (excluding H, N): {B}
    *   Nodes in Path 2 (excluding H, N): {G, F, M, L, E, Y}
    *   Nodes in Path 3 (excluding H, N): {I, C}

    These three sets of intermediate nodes are mutually disjoint. Therefore, there are at least 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and N. According to Menger's Theorem, this means that a minimum of 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect H and N. This rules out any cut set of size 1 or 2.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    Since the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3, I now look for a cut set of size 3. A straightforward way to disconnect a source node from the rest of the graph is to remove all of its direct neighbors (excluding the target node if it's a neighbor, which is not the case here).
    The direct neighbors of H are G, B, and I.
    If I remove nodes G, B, and I:
    *   H will no longer have any outgoing edges to any other node in the graph.
    *   Consequently, no path can exist from H to N (or any other node).

    Therefore, the set [G, B, I] is a valid cut set. It has a size of 3.

6.  **Conclusion:**

    *   Therefore, removing both (J, D) and (P, D) is necessary to disconnect D from the graph.
    *   Since D has a degree of 2 (two incident edges), it is impossible to disconnect it by removing fewer than 2 edges.

6.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect E from D is 2, by isolating node D.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(J, D), (P, D)]

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of non-black nodes to remove to disconnect E and J. This is a minimum vertex cut problem. According to Menger's Theorem (vertex version), the minimum number of vertices whose removal disconnects two non-adjacent vertices is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

I will identify vertex-disjoint paths between E and J. Vertex-disjoint means that these paths do not share any intermediate nodes (nodes other than E and J).

Here are four vertex-disjoint paths I found:
1.  **Path 1:** E - M - J
    *   Intermediate node: {M}
2.  **Path 2:** E - F - K - J
    *   Intermediate nodes: {F, K}
3.  **Path 3:** E - G - H - I - J
    *   Intermediate nodes: {G, H, I}
4.  **Path 4:** E - B - C - O - P - J
    *   Intermediate nodes: {B, C, O, P}

Let's verify that the sets of intermediate nodes for these four paths are indeed disjoint:
*   Set 1: {M}
*   Set 2: {F, K}
*   Set 3: {G, H, I}
*   Set 4: {B, C, O, P}

These four sets are mutually exclusive, meaning the paths are vertex-disjoint.
Since I found 4 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect E and J is at least 4.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 4. A common strategy is to select one intermediate node from each of these vertex-disjoint paths.
Let's choose the following nodes:
*   From Path 1: M
*   From Path 2: F
*   From Path 3: G
*   From Path 4: B

So, the proposed cut set is {M, F, G, B}. Let's verify if removing these four nodes disconnects E from J.

After removing M, F, G, and B, the node E is only connected to L. Node L is only connected to M, which has been removed. Therefore, E becomes completely disconnected from the rest of the graph, including J.

Since we found 4 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 4. We have also identified a cut set of size 4 ({M, F, G, B}) that successfully disconnects E and J. Thus, 4 is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

        Let's verify if this path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2:

        *   Compared to Path 2: Path 2 uses `(E, B)`, `(B, C)`, `(C, I)`, `(I, J)`. Path 3 uses `(H, B)` and `(B, N)`. These are distinct edges. No other common edges.
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

    We have found 3 edge-disjoint paths. This implies that the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

3.  **Attempt to find a fourth edge-disjoint path:**
    *   Edges connected to E that are not used by P1, P2, P3: `(E, L)`.
    *   Edges connected to J that are not used by P1, P2, P3: `(J, D)`.
    *   If a fourth path exists, it would likely use `(E, L)` or `(J, D)`.
    *   Consider a path starting with `(E, L)`: E - L - M - J. This path uses `(M, J)`, which is part of Path 1. So, it's not edge-disjoint.
    *   Consider a path ending with `(J, D)`: J - D. Node D is only connected to J. There is no path from E to D that does not involve J. Thus, `(J, D)` cannot form a fourth edge-disjoint path from E to J.
    *   Since we cannot find a fourth edge-disjoint path, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths is 3.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut set:** Based on Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3. Now, we need to find a specific set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects E and J.

    A straightforward approach is to isolate one of the target nodes by removing its incoming/outgoing edges.
    Consider node J. Its direct connections are `(J, M)`, `(J, D)`, `(J, P)`, `(J, I)`.
    If we remove `(J, M)`, `(J, P)`, and `(J, I)`, then J is only connected to D.
    Let's verify if E can reach J (or D) after removing these three edges:
    *   Any path from E to J through M is broken by `(J, M)`. (e.g., E-M-J, E-L-M-J)
    *   Any path from E to J through P is broken by `(J, P)`. (e.g., E-B-N-Y-O-P-J, E-F-X-K-G-H-B-N-Y-O-P-J)

    *   The only remaining connection for J is `(J, D)`. However, D is only connected to J. There are no paths from E to D that do not involve J. Therefore, E cannot reach D, and thus cannot reach J.

    This cut set `[(J, M), (J, P), (J, I)]` successfully disconnects E and J, and its size is 3, which is the minimum possible.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are J and F.
2.  **Understand the goal:** Determine the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect J and F.
3.  **Initial assessment of simple cuts:**
    *   To disconnect J from the rest of the graph, we can remove all edges connected to J. These are (J, M), (J, D), (J, P), and (J, I). This would require removing 4 edges.
    *   To disconnect F from the rest of the graph, we can remove all edges connected to F. These are (F, E), (F, B), (F, K), (F, X), and (F, G). This would require removing 5 edges.
    *   From this, we know the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 4.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Edge Version):** The minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes (s, t) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between s and t. If we can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size is at least *k*.

5.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths from J to F:**
    Let's try to find as many edge-disjoint paths as possible.

    *   **Path 1:** J - M - L - E - F
        Edges: (J, M), (M, L), (L, E), (E, F)

    *   **Path 2:** J - D - P - Y - N - B - F
        Edges: (J, D), (D, P), (P, Y), (Y, N), (N, B), (B, F)

    *   **Path 3:** J - P - O - C - H - X - F
        Edges: (J, P), (P, O), (O, C), (C, H), (H, X), (X, F)

    *   **Path 4:** J - I - H - G - F
        Edges: (J, I), (I, H), (H, G), (G, F)

6.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    Let's list all edges used in these four paths and check for any overlaps:
    *   **Path 1 Edges:** (J, M), (M, L), (L, E), (E, F)
    *   **Path 2 Edges:** (J, D), (D, P), (P, Y), (Y, N), (N, B), (B, F)
    *   **Path 3 Edges:** (J, P), (P, O), (O, C), (C, H), (H, X), (X, F)
    *   **Path 4 Edges:** (J, I), (I, H), (H, G), (G, F)

    Let's check for common edges:
    *   **Edges directly from J:** (J, M), (J, D), (J, P), (J, I) are all distinct. (No overlap)
    *   **Edges from M:** (M, L) is only in Path 1. (M, O) is not used in any of these paths. (No overlap)
    *   **Edges from D:** (D, P) is only in Path 2. (No overlap)
    *   **Edges from P:** (P, Y) is in Path 2, (P, O) is in Path 3. These are distinct. (P, N) is not used. (No overlap)
    *   **Edges from I:** (I, H) is in Path 4. (I, C) is not used. (No overlap)
    *   **Edges from L:** (L, E) is only in Path 1. (No overlap)
    *   **Edges from Y:** (Y, N) is only in Path 2. (Y, O) is not used. (No overlap)
    *   **Edges from N:** (N, B) is only in Path 2. (N, O), (N, C) are not used. (No overlap)
    *   **Edges from O:** (O, C) is in Path 3. (O, B), (O, N), (O, Y) are not used. (No overlap)
    *   **Edges from C:** (C, H) is in Path 3. (C, B) is not used. (No overlap)
    *   **Edges from H:** (H, X) is in Path 3, (H, G) is in Path 4. These are distinct. (No overlap)
    *   **Edges directly to F:** (E, F) in Path 1, (B, F) in Path 2, (X, F) in Path 3, (G, F) in Path 4. These are all distinct edges. (No overlap)

    All edges in these four paths are distinct. Therefore, these are 4 edge-disjoint paths.

7.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we found 4 edge-disjoint paths between J and F, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect J and F is at least 4.
    We already identified a cut set of size 4 by removing all edges directly connected to J: [(J, M), (J, D), (J, P), (J, I)]. This set successfully disconnects J from the rest of the graph, and thus from F.
    Since the minimum cut is at least 4, and we found a cut of size 4, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are F and D.
2.  **Goal:** Determine the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect F and D.
3.  **Analyze Node D's Connections:** Node D is directly connected to two other nodes: J and P.
    *   Edge (J, D)
    *   Edge (P, D)
4.  **Consider Paths to D:** Any path originating from F and aiming to reach D must necessarily pass through either node J or node P, and then use the respective edge (J, D) or (P, D) to finally arrive at D.
5.  **Evaluate Cut Set Options:**
    *   If we remove only the edge (J, D), paths from F to D via P would still exist (e.g., F-E-H-I-P-D).
    *   If we remove only the edge (P, D), paths from F to D via J would still exist (e.g., F-E-M-J-D).
    *   Therefore, to ensure no path exists between F and D, both edges (J, D) and (P, D) must be removed.
6.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** Removing both (J, D) and (P, D) completely isolates node D from the rest of the graph, thus ensuring no path from F can reach D. This set contains 2 edges. Since removing only one edge is insufficient, 2 is the minimum number of edges required.

**Alternative Cut Set (from F's side):**
1.  **Analyze Node F's Connections:** Node F is directly connected to K, X, G, and E.
2.  **Identify Critical Outgoing Edges from F's Cluster:** To leave the immediate cluster formed by F, K, X, and G, any path from F must use either the edge (F, E) or the edge (G, H) (since G is connected to H, and K and X are only connected to F and G within this cluster).
3.  **Evaluate Cut Set:** If we remove (F, E) and (G, H):

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are X and C. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect X from C.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node X is connected to F, K, G.
    *   Node C is connected to B, O, I, N, Y.

2.  **Consider simple cut sets:**
    *   Removing all edges connected to X: (X, F), (X, K), (X, G). This is a cut set of size 3. If these edges are removed, X is isolated, and thus disconnected from C.
    *   Removing all edges connected to C: (C, B), (C, O), (C, I), (C, N), (C, Y). This is a cut set of size 5. If these edges are removed, C is isolated, and thus disconnected from X.
    *   Since a cut set of size 3 exists, the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 3.

3.  **Attempt to find a smaller cut set (size 1 or 2):**
    *   It's clear that no single edge removal can disconnect X and C, as there are multiple distinct paths.
    *   Let's try to find if a cut set of size 2 exists. This means that for any pair of edges removed, there must still be a path between X and C. This is often hard to prove directly.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Edge Version):** The minimum number of edges in an s-t cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between s and t. If I can find 3 edge-disjoint paths between X and C, then the minimum cut size must be at least 3. Since I already found a cut of size 3, this would prove it's the minimum.

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from X to C:
    *   **Path 1:** X - G - B - C
        Edges: (X, G), (G, B), (B, C)

    *   **Path 2:** X - F - E - L - M - J - P - O - C
        Edges: (X, F), (F, E), (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, C)

    *   **Path 3:** X - K - G - H - I - C
        Edges: (X, K), (K, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, C)

    Now, let's check if these three paths are edge-disjoint (i.e., they do not share any common edges):
    *   Edges in Path 1: {(X, G), (G, B), (B, C)}
    *   Edges in Path 2: {(X, F), (F, E), (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, C)}
    *   Edges in Path 3: {(X, K), (K, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, C)}

    Upon inspection, all edges listed for Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are unique and do not overlap. For example, (X,G) is distinct from (X,F) and (X,K). (G,B) is distinct from (G,H). (B,C) is distinct from (O,C) and (I,C).
    Therefore, we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between X and C.

5.  **Conclusion based on Menger's Theorem:**
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect X and C. We have already identified a cut set of size 3: [(X, F), (X, K), (X, G)] or [(B, C), (I, C), (O, C)]. Both are valid minimal cut sets.

    Let's choose [(B, C), (I, C), (O, C)] as the cut set and verify its effectiveness:
    *   If (B, C), (I, C), and (O, C) are removed, node C is left connected only to N and Y.
    *   Node N is connected to B and O. Since (B, C) and (O, C) are removed, N cannot reach C via B or O.
    *   Node Y is connected to O. Since (O, C) is removed, Y cannot reach C via O.
    *   Therefore, C becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, ensuring no path exists between X and C.

The minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, C), (I, C), (O, C)]

    *   For example, removing (F, E) still leaves paths like F-G-H-I-J.
    *   Removing (G, H) still leaves paths like F-E-M-J.
    *   Removing (M, J) still leaves paths like F-E-I-J or F-G-H-B-N-O-P-J.
    *   It's clear that no single edge removal can disconnect F and J, as there are multiple independent routes. Therefore, the minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

3.  **Explore potential cut sets of size 2:**
    I will look for a "bottleneck" or a set of edges that all paths from F to J must cross.
    Let's consider the nodes directly connected to F: {X, K, G, E}.
    Notice that nodes X, K, G, and F form a tightly connected cluster:
    *   F is connected to X, K, G.
    *   X is connected to F, K, G.
    *   K is connected to F, X, G.
    *   G is connected to F, X, K.
    This means that F, X, K, G form a connected component.

    *   From F, the edge (F, E) connects to node E, which is outside this cluster.
    *   From G, the edge (G, H) connects to node H, which is outside this cluster.
    *   Nodes X and K have no connections outside the {F, X, K, G} cluster other than to F and G.

    Therefore, all paths originating from F and going towards J (which is outside the {F, X, K, G} cluster) must pass through either the edge (F, E) or the edge (G, H).

4.  **Verify the proposed cut set `[(F, E), (G, H)]`:**
    *   If the edge (F, E) is removed, all paths that start from F and go through E are broken.
    *   If the edge (G, H) is removed, all paths that start from F and go through G (or X, or K, which then connect to G) are broken, because G's only connection to the rest of the graph (outside the F,X,K,G cluster) is through H.

    Let's confirm this:
    *   Any path F -> E -> ... -> J is broken by removing (F, E).
    *   Any path F -> G -> H -> ... -> J is broken by removing (G, H).
    *   Any path F -> X -> G -> H -> ... -> J is broken by removing (G, H).
    *   Any path F -> K -> G -> H -> ... -> J is broken by removing (G, H).

    Since all possible paths from F to J must use either (F, E) or (G, H) as their "exit point" from the {F, X, K, G} cluster, removing these two edges will completely disconnect F from J.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    The cut set `[(F, E), (G, H)]` consists of 2 edges. Since we determined that 1 edge is not sufficient, 2 edges is the minimum.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect all paths between E and J.

1.  **Identify direct connections of E and J:**
    *   Neighbors of E: F, G, H, L, M
    *   Neighbors of J: M, I, P, D

2.  **Systematically explore paths from E to J:**
    I will consider the main "gateways" from E towards J. These are the nodes directly connected to E that can lead to J.

    *   **Path through M:**
        *   E - M - J
        *   E - L - M - J

    *   **Paths through H:**
        *   E - H - I - J
        *   E - H - I - P - J
        *   E - H - B - C - I - J
        *   E - H - B - C - O - P - J
        *   E - H - B - N - O - P - J
        *   (Note: Paths starting E-F-G-H... or E-G-H... also fall into this category, as they eventually pass through H to reach J's side of the graph.)

    *   **Paths through L:**
        *   E - L - M - J (already covered under paths through M)

    *   **Paths through F or G:**
        *   E - F - G - H - ... (These paths eventually lead to H, which then leads to J's neighborhood).
        *   E - G - H - ... (These paths also eventually lead to H).

3.  **Identify potential cut edges:**
    To disconnect E from J, we need to cut all these paths. Let's look for a minimal set of edges.

    Consider the edges that are critical for these main branches:
    *   For paths through M: (E, M) or (M, J) or (E, L) and (L, M).
    *   For paths through H: (E, H) or (H, I) or (H, B) or (I, J) or (P, J) etc.

4.  **Test for a cut set of size 1:**
    It's clear that removing only one edge will not disconnect E and J, as there are multiple distinct routes. For example, if we remove (M, J), paths through H (like E-H-I-J) still exist. If we remove (E, H), paths through M (like E-M-J) still exist.

5.  **Test for a cut set of size 2:**
    Let's try to find a set of two edges whose removal disconnects E and J.

    Consider removing the edges **(E, H)** and **(M, J)**.
    Let's verify if this set disconnects E and J:

    *   **Effect of removing (E, H):**
        This edge is a direct connection from E to H. By removing it, all paths from E that rely on H as the next step are immediately broken. This includes:
        *   E - H - I - J
        *   E - H - I - P - J
        *   E - H - B - C - I - J
        *   E - H - B - C - O - P - J
        *   E - H - B - N - O - P - J
        *   Furthermore, any paths from E through F or G (e.g., E-F-G-H-...) are also blocked from reaching H, and thus J, because H is the only gateway from the F/G cluster to J's side of the graph.

    *   **Effect of removing (M, J):**
        This edge is a direct connection from M to J. By removing it, all paths from E that rely on M as the final step to J are immediately broken. This includes:
        *   E - M - J
        *   E - L - M - J

    *   **Are there any remaining paths?**
        After removing (E, H) and (M, J), let's check E's remaining connections:
        *   E is connected to F, G, L, M.
        *   From F: F connects to G, K, X. All these eventually lead to H. But (E, H) is removed, so E cannot reach H via F or G.
        *   From G: G connects to F, K, X, H. But (E, H) is removed, so E cannot reach H via G.
        *   From L: L connects to M. M connects to J. But (M, J) is removed. So E-L-M-J is broken.
        *   From M: M connects to J. But (M, J) is removed. So E-M-J is broken.

    Since all identified paths from E to J are broken by removing these two edges, and there are no other ways for E to reach J, this cut set of size 2 is effective.

6.  **Confirm minimality:**
    Since we've shown that 1 edge is not enough, and we found a cut set of 2 edges, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2. This is consistent with Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths. We can identify two edge-disjoint paths:
    *   Path 1: E - M - J
    *   Path 2: E - H - I - P - J
    These two paths do not share any common edges. It is not possible to find a third edge-disjoint path.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(E, H), (M, J)]

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects N from H.

6.  **Verify the cut set:** The cut set [(N, O), (N, C), (N, B)] consists of 3 edges. Removing these edges isolates N from all possible routes to H.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths (implying a minimum cut of at least 3) and identified a cut set of size 3 that successfully disconnects N from H, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and H. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and H. I cannot remove E or H.

First, I identify all direct and indirect paths between E and H.
I can identify three primary, largely node-disjoint paths or "channels" connecting E and H:

1.  **Path 1: E - B - H**
    *   This path uses node B.

2.  **Path 2: E - I - H**
    *   This path uses node I.

3.  **Path 3: E - F - G - H**
    *   This path uses nodes F and G.

To disconnect E and H, I must break each of these three paths.
*   To break Path 1 (E - B - H), I must remove node B.
*   To break Path 2 (E - I - H), I must remove node I.
*   To break Path 3 (E - F - G - H), I must remove either node F or node G.

Since B, I, F, and G are all distinct nodes, these three paths are essentially node-disjoint (except for the start and end nodes E and H). Therefore, to break all three, I need to remove at least one node from each "bottleneck" point. This implies a minimum of 3 nodes must be removed.

Let's propose a cut set of 3 nodes: [B, I, G].

Now, I will verify if removing these three nodes (B, I, G) indeed disconnects E and H, and that no smaller set would suffice.

**Verification of [B, I, G]:**
1.  **E - B - H:** This path is broken because B is removed.
2.  **E - I - H:** This path is broken because I is removed.
3.  **E - F - G - H:** This path is broken because G is removed.

Now, I need to check all other possible paths from E to H, ensuring they are also broken by the removal of B, I, and G.
Let's trace from E to its neighbors, avoiding B, I, and G:
*   **From E to F:** F is connected to K, X. Neither K nor X are connected to H or any of its remaining neighbors (since B, I, G are removed). So, no path from E-F to H.
*   **From E to L:** L is connected to M.
    *   E - L - M. M is connected to J.
    *   E - L - M - J. J is connected to D, P.
        *   J - D - P. P is connected to J, I, C, O. Since I is removed, P cannot reach H via I. P can reach C and O. C is connected to N, O. O is connected to C, N, P, Y. N is connected to B, C, O, Y. Since B and I are removed, there is no path from C, O, N, Y to H.
        *   J - P. P is connected to J, I, C, O. Similar to above, I is removed, and C, O, N, Y cannot reach H because B and I are removed.
    *   Therefore, no path from E-L to H.
*   **From E to M:** M is connected to L, J.
    *   E - M - L: This path is covered by the E-L analysis above, which showed no connection to H.
    *   E - M - J: This path is covered by the E-L-M-J analysis above, which showed no connection to H.
    *   Therefore, no path from E-M to H.

All paths from E to H are broken by removing nodes B, I, and G.

**Minimum Number of Nodes:**
As established, there are three distinct "channels" (E-B-H, E-I-H, E-F-G-H) that are node-disjoint except for the target nodes. To break all three, at least one node from each channel must be removed. This requires a minimum of 3 nodes.
For example, if we tried to remove only 2 nodes:
*   Removing [B, I] would leave E - F - G - H intact.
*   Removing [B, G] would leave E - I - H intact.
*   Removing [I, G] would leave E - B - H intact.
Thus, 2 nodes are not sufficient.

Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3. The cut set can be [B, I, G] or [B, I, F].

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, I, G]

Image name: PGS_task3_gt3_PAIR2

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 11
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and H (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from H.

1.  **Identify the direct neighbors of the target nodes:**
    *   Neighbors of E: L, M, F, B
    *   Neighbors of H: G, B, I

2.  **Identify all possible paths between E and H:**
    To find the minimum edge cut, I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. I will look for edge-disjoint paths from E to H.

    *   **Path 1 (P1):** E - B - H
        Edges: (E, B), (B, H)

    *   **Path 2 (P2):** E - F - G - H
        Edges: (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)
        This path is edge-disjoint from P1 as it uses different edges.

    *   **Path 3 (P3):** E - L - M - F - K - J - I - H
        Edges: (E, L), (L, M), (M, F), (F, K), (K, J), (J, I), (I, H)
        Let's check if this path is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2:
        *   P1 edges: {(E, B), (B, H)}
        *   P2 edges: {(E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}
        *   P3 edges: {(E, L), (L, M), (M, F), (F, K), (K, J), (J, I), (I, H)}
        All edges in P3 are distinct from the edges in P1 and P2. Therefore, P1, P2, and P3 are three edge-disjoint paths.

3.  **Determine the minimum number of edges to remove:**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect H from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to H.
    The edges connected to H are: (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).
    Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects E from H:
    *   Removing (B, H) breaks P1 (E - B - H).
    *   Removing (G, H) breaks P2 (E - F - G - H) and any other path going through G to H (e.g., E - F - X - G - H).
    *   Removing (I, H) breaks P3 (E - L - M - F - K - J - I - H) and any other path going through I to H (e.g., E - B - C - I - H).
    By removing these three edges, node H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, ensuring no path exists between E and H.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3. We also found a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects E and H. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are X and C. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect X from C. I cannot remove X or C.

2.  **Analyze Connections to Target Nodes:**
    *   Neighbors of X: F, K, G
    *   Neighbors of C: B, O, I, N

3.  **Search for Vertex-Disjoint Paths:** To find the minimum cut set, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between X and C. If I can find *k* vertex-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut set size is at least *k*.

    Let's try to find three vertex-disjoint paths (excluding X and C):

    *   **Path 1:** X -> F -> E -> B -> C
        *   Nodes in this path (excluding X, C): {F, E, B}

    *   **Path 2:** X -> G -> H -> I -> C
        *   Nodes in this path (excluding X, C): {G, H, I}

    *   **Path 3:** X -> K -> J -> P -> O -> C
        *   Nodes in this path (excluding X, C): {K, J, P, O}

4.  **Verify Vertex-Disjoint Property:**
    *   Comparing Path 1 ({F, E, B}) with Path 2 ({G, H, I}): No common nodes.
    *   Comparing Path 1 ({F, E, B}) with Path 3 ({K, J, P, O}): No common nodes.
    *   Comparing Path 2 ({G, H, I}) with Path 3 ({K, J, P, O}): No common nodes.

    Since I have found three vertex-disjoint paths between X and C, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect X and C must be at least 3.

5.  **Identify a Candidate Cut Set of Size 3:**
    Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects X and C. A good strategy is to consider the direct neighbors of C (B, O, I, N) and see if removing a subset of these, or nodes immediately "upstream" of them, can achieve disconnection.

    Let's consider removing the nodes {B, I, O}. These are three of C's direct neighbors.

6.  **Verify the Cut Set {B, I, O}:**
    If nodes B, I, and O are removed:

    *   **Paths to C via B:** Any path that uses the edge (B, C) is broken because B is removed. This includes paths like X-F-E-B-C, X-F-G-B-C, X-G-B-C.
    *   **Paths to C via I:** Any path that uses the edge (I, C) is broken because I is removed. This includes paths like X-F-G-H-I-C, X-G-H-I-C, X-F-K-J-P-I-C, X-K-J-P-I-C.
    *   **Paths to C via O:** Any path that uses the edge (O, C) is broken because O is removed. This includes paths like X-F-E-L-M-J-P-O-C, X-F-K-J-P-O-C, X-K-J-P-O-C.
    *   **Paths to C via N:** Node N is connected to C, B, and Y.
        *   If N tries to reach X's side via B (e.g., X-...-B-N-C), this path is broken because B is removed.
        *   If N tries to reach X's side via Y (e.g., X-...-N-Y-O-C), this path is broken because O is removed (Y is only connected to N and O).
        *   Therefore, with B and O removed, N becomes isolated from X's side, and no path can reach C through N.

    Since all direct connections to C (via B, I, O, N) are severed by removing {B, I, O}, X and C are completely disconnected.

7.  **Conclusion:** I found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, meaning at least 3 nodes must be removed. I also found a cut set of size 3 ({B, I, O}) that successfully disconnects X and C. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, E and J.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Target Node E is connected to: L, M, F, B.
    *   Target Node J is connected to: D, K, I, P.

2.  **Analyze the connections of J:**
    *   Node D is only connected to J. There are no other edges connected to D. This means that no path from E can reach D, and therefore, the edge (D, J) is not part of any path from E to J. Removing (D, J) would not contribute to disconnecting E from J.
    *   Therefore, all paths from E to J must pass through K, I, or P to reach J.

3.  **Consider a cut set by isolating J:**
    To completely disconnect J from E, we need to remove all edges connecting J to nodes that are reachable from E. These nodes are K, I, and P.
    *   Edge (K, J)
    *   Edge (I, J)
    *   Edge (P, J)
    If these three edges are removed, J will only be connected to D. Since D is not connected to E (or any other node that could lead to E), J becomes completely isolated from E.
    This cut set, `[(K, J), (I, J), (P, J)]`, has a size of 3.

4.  **Check for a smaller cut (size 1 or 2):**
    *   **Size 1:** A single edge removal would disconnect E and J. Let's test some critical edges:
        *   Removing (E, F) still leaves paths like E-B-...-J.
        *   Removing (E, B) still leaves paths like E-F-...-J.
        *   Removing (K, J) still leaves paths like E-...-I-J or E-...-P-J.
        *   Removing (I, J) still leaves paths like E-...-K-J or E-...-P-J.
        *   Removing (P, J) still leaves paths like E-...-K-J or E-...-I-J.
        No single edge removal disconnects E and J. Thus, the minimum cut size is at least 2.

    *   **Size 2:** We need to determine if there are two edges whose removal would disconnect E and J. This is equivalent to checking if there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J (by Menger's Theorem). If there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut is at least 3.

    Let's try to find 3 edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - F - K - J
        Edges: `{(E, F), (F, K), (K, J)}`

    *   **Path 2:** E - B - H - I - J
        Edges: `{(E, B), (B, H), (H, I), (I, J)}`
        (Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint as they use different initial edges from E (E-F vs E-B) and different final edges to J (K-J vs I-J), and the intermediate nodes/edges are also distinct.)

    *   **Path 3:** We need a path from E to J that does not use any of the edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
        Edges used by P1 and P2: `{(E, F), (F, K), (K, J), (E, B), (B, H), (H, I), (I, J)}`
        Let's try to construct a third path:
        E - M - F - G - H - B - N - O - P - J
        Let's check the edges of this candidate Path 3:
        `{(E, M), (M, F), (F, G), (G, H), (H, B), (B, N), (N, O), (O, P), (P, J)}`
        Comparing with Path 1: No common edges.
        Comparing with Path 2: This path uses (H, B). Path 2 uses (B, H). These are the same edge. Therefore, this path is NOT edge-disjoint from Path 2.

    Let's try another candidate for Path 3:
    E - B - C - O - P - J
    This path uses (E, B) which is part of Path 2. So it's not edge-disjoint.

    Let's try to find a path that uses (E,M) and ends at (P,J) without using any edges from P1 or P2.
    E - M - F - G - X - K - J (uses (K,J) from P1) - Not disjoint.
    E - M - F - G - H - I - J (uses (I,J) from P2) - Not disjoint.

    It is challenging to find 3 completely edge-disjoint paths by inspection, which often indicates that the minimum cut is indeed 3.
    Given that removing any two of the three critical edges connected to J (K, I, P) still leaves a path, it strongly suggests that a cut of size 2 is not possible.
    For example:
    *   Removing `(K, J)` and `(I, J)`: Paths via `P` still exist (e.g., E-B-N-O-P-J).
    *   Removing `(K, J)` and `(P, J)`: Paths via `I` still exist (e.g., E-B-H-I-J).
    *   Removing `(I, J)` and `(P, J)`: Paths via `K` still exist (e.g., E-F-K-J).

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since a cut of size 2 is not sufficient, and we have identified a valid cut of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

            *   E - F - G - H - ...
            *   E - G - X - F - H - ...
            *   E - F - X - G - H - ...
            The nodes G, F, and X form a sub-component with E that is connected to the rest of the graph primarily through H (and M, L, which are covered by the 'M' path group). Specifically, if M and L are considered, the only way for E to reach the "J-side" of the graph via G, F, or X is by eventually passing through H.

5.  **Identify critical nodes:**
    Based on the path analysis, it becomes clear that all paths from E to J must pass through one of two "bottleneck" nodes:
    *   **Node M**: This node is directly connected to both E (and L, which connects to E) and J.
    *   **Node H**: This node is directly connected to E (and G, F, X, which connect to E) and serves as the gateway to the network of nodes (B, C, I, O, P) that connect to J.

6.  **Formulate a cut set:**
    To disconnect E and J, I must remove both M and H.
    *   Removing M will break all paths that go E -> M -> J and E -> L -> M -> J.
    *   Removing H will break all paths that go E -> H -> ... -> J, and also all paths that go E -> G/F/X -> H -> ... -> J.

7.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   If I remove only M, paths like E - H - I - J would still exist, connecting E and J.
    *   If I remove only H, paths like E - M - J would still exist, connecting E and J.
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient to disconnect E and J.
    *   Since a cut set of size 2 ([M, H]) has been found, and a cut set of size 1 is not possible, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [M, H]

*   H - X - K - F - E - B

*   **Edge 1: (H, C)** - Removing this edge breaks all paths that start directly from H to C (Category 1 paths). It also breaks any paths that might have branched from H to C.
*   **Edge 2: (H, I)** - Removing this edge breaks all paths that start directly from H to I (Category 2 paths). At this point, all paths from H that go through C or I are broken. The only remaining paths must start from H to G or H to X.
*   **Edge 3: (E, B)** - After removing (H, C) and (H, I), the only remaining paths from H to B are those in Category 3 (starting H-G or H-X). All these paths converge and pass through the sequence F - E - B. By removing the edge (E, B), all these remaining paths are broken.

Now, I must verify that this is the *minimum* number of edges. I will attempt to find a cut set with 2 edges.

*   Branch 1: Through C (via H-C or H-I-C)
*   Branch 2: Through I (not C, via J/D/P/M/O/N)
*   Branch 3: Through G/X (via K/F/E)

3.  If I remove (H, I) and (E, B): Paths from Category 2 and 3 are broken. However, paths from Category 1, such as H - C - B, still exist. So, 2 edges are not enough.

    *   **Size 2:** Let's assume a cut of size 2 exists. This implies that all paths from J to E must pass through at least one of these two edges. This is often best proven by finding 3 edge-disjoint paths.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are H and O. The goal is to disconnect H from O by removing the minimum number of white nodes.

2.  **Identify vertex-disjoint paths:** To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between H and O. By Menger's Theorem, this number is equal to the minimum vertex cut size.
    *   **Path 1:** H -> I -> C -> O
        *   Intermediate nodes: {I, C}
    *   **Path 2:** H -> G -> K -> J -> P -> O
        *   Intermediate nodes: {G, K, J, P}
    *   **Path 3:** H -> B -> N -> O
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B, N}

3.  **Verify vertex-disjoint nature:** I check if the sets of intermediate nodes for these three paths are mutually disjoint:
    *   {I, C} ∩ {G, K, J, P} = {}
    *   {I, C} ∩ {B, N} = {}
    *   {G, K, J, P} ∩ {B, N} = {}
    Since the sets of intermediate nodes are disjoint, these three paths are indeed vertex-disjoint (except for the source H and sink O).

4.  **Determine minimum cut size:** Because there are at least 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and O, Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect H and O is at least 3.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** I now need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects H and O.
    *   Consider the direct neighbors of node O: C, P, and Y.
    *   If I remove nodes C, P, and Y, node O will have no remaining connections to any other node in the graph.
    *   None of these nodes (C, P, Y) are the target nodes H or O.
    *   Therefore, removing the set {C, P, Y} will completely disconnect O from H (and from the rest of the graph).

6.  **Conclusion:** Since the minimum cut size is at least 3, and I have found a valid cut set of size 3 ({C, P, Y}), this is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [C, P, Y]

Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between E and J.

First, I identify all nodes directly connected to the target node J. These are the "entry points" to J from the rest of the graph.
Neighbors of J: M, I, P, D.
So, any path from E to J must pass through one of the edges (M, J), (I, J), (P, J), or (D, J).

Next, I need to determine if node D is reachable from E.
D is connected to J and P.
P is connected to I, J, D, O.
O is connected to C, N, P, Y.
Y is connected to O.
C is connected to B, I, N, O.
B is connected to E, H, C, N.
Since E is connected to B, and B is connected to C, and C is connected to O, and O is connected to P, and P is connected to D, it means D is reachable from E (e.g., E-B-C-O-P-D). However, the edge (D, J) is not a critical edge for disconnecting E and J because if we cut all other paths to J, D itself would be disconnected from E's component. The question is about paths *between* E and J. If D is only connected to J and P, and P is already covered by (P, J), then (D, J) is not a primary "bottleneck" for paths from E to J. All paths from E to J must eventually pass through M, I, or P to reach J.

Let's list the distinct paths from E to J, categorized by the last node before J:

1.  **Paths through M to J:**
    *   E - M - J
    *   E - L - M - J
    All these paths use the edge (M, J).

2.  **Paths through I to J:**
    *   E - F - G - H - I - J
    *   E - F - X - G - H - I - J
    *   E - F - K - X - G - H - I - J
    *   E - B - H - I - J
    *   E - B - C - I - J
    *   E - B - N - C - I - J
    All these paths use the edge (I, J).

3.  **Paths through P to J:**
    *   E - B - C - O - P - J
    *   E - B - N - C - O - P - J
    *   E - B - N - O - P - J
    All these paths use the edge (P, J).

To completely disconnect E from J, we must ensure that no path exists between them. This means we must cut at least one edge from each of the identified path categories.
The edges (M, J), (I, J), and (P, J) are the only direct connections from the rest of the graph (reachable from E) to J.
If we remove any two of these three edges, a path will still exist through the remaining edge:
*   If (M, J) and (I, J) are removed, paths like E - B - C - O - P - J still exist.
*   If (M, J) and (P, J) are removed, paths like E - B - C - I - J still exist.
*   If (I, J) and (P, J) are removed, paths like E - M - J still exist.

Therefore, to break all paths between E and J, all three edges (M, J), (I, J), and (P, J) must be removed. This constitutes a cut set of size 3.

I have verified that removing these three edges breaks all existing paths between E and J. I have also explored the graph systematically and confirmed that no smaller set of edges can achieve the disconnection.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, J), (I, J), (P, J)]

    *   **To isolate L:** L has two incident edges: (L, M) and (L, E). If these two edges are removed, L becomes completely disconnected from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from L to J. This cut set is [(L, M), (L, E)], with a size of 2.

    *   Isolating L requires 2 edges.

4.  **Determine the minimum:**

5.  **Verify no smaller cut exists:**

If (J, M) is removed, paths through (J, I) and (J, P) still exist.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are N (black) and G (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect N from G.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node N is connected to: Y, O, C, B.
    *   Node G is connected to: H, K, F, X.

2.  **Analyze the structure around node G:**
    Nodes K, F, and X are closely connected to G. Let's consider the subgraph formed by {G, K, F, X}.
    *   G is connected to K, F, X.
    *   K is connected to G, F, X.
    *   F is connected to G, K, X.
    *   X is connected to G, K, F.
    This forms a tightly connected component (a clique of 4 nodes if all were connected, but here it's a dense subgraph).

3.  **Identify all edges that connect this {G, K, F, X} cluster to the rest of the graph:**
    *   Edge (H, G): Node H is outside the {G, K, F, X} cluster.
    *   Edge (P, F): Node P is outside the {G, K, F, X} cluster.
    *   All other edges involving G, K, F, or X (e.g., (G, K), (G, F), (G, X), (K, F), (K, X), (F, X)) are internal to this cluster.

4.  **Determine the cut set:**
    Any path from N to G must eventually enter the {G, K, F, X} cluster. The only two "entry points" (edges) from the rest of the graph into this cluster are (H, G) and (P, F).
    *   If we remove the edge (H, G), any path that goes through H to reach G will be broken. For example, N-C-H-G.
    *   If we remove the edge (P, F), any path that goes through P to reach F (and then G) will be broken. For example, N-O-P-F-G.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   If we remove only (H, G), paths like N-O-P-F-G would still exist.
    *   If we remove only (P, F), paths like N-C-H-G would still exist.
    Therefore, both edges (H, G) and (P, F) are necessary to completely disconnect N from G.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    Removing the two edges (H, G) and (P, F) will ensure that no path exists between N and G. This is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(H, G), (P, F)]

    *   Node G is connected to K, X, and H. If we remove the three edges (G, K), (G, X), and (G, H), node G becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from N. This is also a cut set of size 3.
    Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 3.

3.  **Check if a cut set of size 1 is possible:**
    A cut set of size 1 implies a "bridge" edge, meaning all paths between N and G must pass through that single edge. By visual inspection, there are clearly multiple distinct routes between N and G, so removing any single edge will not disconnect them. Thus, the minimum is not 1.

4.  **Check if a cut set of size 2 is possible:**
    To determine if 2 edges are sufficient, we need to see if there are at least three edge-disjoint paths between N and G. If there are three or more edge-disjoint paths, then at least three edges must be removed.
    Let's identify some paths between N and G:
    *   **Path A:** N - B - H - G. Edges: {(N,B), (B,H), (H,G)}
    *   **Path B:** N - O - P - I - C - B - E - F - K - G. Edges: {(N,O), (O,P), (P,I), (I,C), (C,B), (B,E), (E,F), (F,K), (K,G)}

    *   Path A and Path B are edge-disjoint (they share no common edges).
    *   Path A and Path C are edge-disjoint (they share no common edges).
    *   Path B and Path C are NOT edge-disjoint (they share edges like (O,P), (P,I), (I,C), (C,B), (B,E), (E,F)).

    Since Path A is edge-disjoint from both Path B and Path C, and Path B and Path C are not edge-disjoint, we cannot immediately conclude that there are 3 edge-disjoint paths.

    However, we can demonstrate that 2 edges are not enough by finding a scenario where removing two edges still leaves a path.
    Consider removing two edges that would break Path A and Path B:
    Let's remove (B,H) (from Path A) and (K,G) (from Path B).

1.  **Identify all direct neighbors of the target nodes:**
    *   Neighbors of E: M, L, F, B
    *   Neighbors of H: B, G, I

2.  **Identify potential "bottleneck" nodes or edges:**
    All paths from E to H must eventually pass through one of H's direct neighbors: B, G, or I. This suggests that the edges connecting to H, namely (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H), are critical.

3.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths:**
    To determine the minimum number of edges required, we can look for the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H (Menger's Theorem).
    Let's try to find three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - B - H
        Edges: {(E, B), (B, H)}
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - G - H
        Edges: {(E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}
    *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - P - I - H
        Edges: {(E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, H)}

    Let's check if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   Edges in Path 1: (E, B), (B, H)
    *   Edges in Path 2: (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)
    *   Edges in Path 3: (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, H)

    There are no common edges among these three sets. Therefore, we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect E and H is at least 3.

4.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**
    Consider removing all edges directly connected to H that lead to the rest of the graph:
    *   (B, H)
    *   (G, H)
    *   (I, H)

5.  **Verify the cut set:**
    If we remove these three edges:
    *   The edge (B, H) cuts all paths from E that go through B and then directly to H (e.g., E-B-H). It also cuts paths that go E-B-...-I-H if they use (B,H) as their final step to H, but in this case, (I,H) would be the final step.
    *   The edge (G, H) cuts all paths from E that go through G and then directly to H (e.g., E-F-G-H, E-F-X-K-G-H).
    *   The edge (I, H) cuts all paths from E that go through I and then directly to H (e.g., E-M-J-P-I-H, E-B-N-C-I-H, etc.).

    By removing these three edges, node H becomes completely isolated from all other nodes in the graph, including E. Since we have established that the minimum number of edges is at least 3, and we found a cut set of size 3 that achieves disconnection, this is the minimal cut set.

    *   This path uses node C.

    *   This path uses nodes I, J, D, P, Y.

    *   Comparing with Path 1 and Path 2, this path is node-disjoint from both (except for H and N).

Now, I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects H and N. I can pick one node from each of the identified node-disjoint paths (excluding H and N).

*   From Path 1 (H - C - N), I choose node **C**.
*   From Path 2 (H - I - J - D - P - Y - N), I choose node **J**.
*   From Path 3 (H - G - X - K - F - E - B - N), I choose node **E**.

1.  **Paths through C**: Any path H-C-N is broken by removing C.
2.  **Paths through I**: All paths starting from H via I must pass through J (e.g., H-I-J-M-O-N, H-I-J-D-P-O-N, H-I-J-D-P-Y-N). Removing J breaks all these paths.

Since all identified paths and any other potential paths would have to pass through one of these bottleneck nodes (C, J, or E), removing them ensures complete disconnection between H and N.

Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Image name: FD-K_task2_gt2_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 11
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The goal is to find the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect the two black nodes, B and M, without removing B or M themselves.

First, I identify the two black nodes: B and M.

Next, I identify all possible paths from B to M. Any path from B to M must eventually reach one of M's direct neighbors: E, L, or J.

Let's analyze the connections to M:
1.  **Via E**: M is directly connected to E.
2.  **Via L**: M is directly connected to L. L's only other connection is to E. This means any path to L (and then to M) must pass through E (i.e., B -> ... -> E -> L -> M).
3.  **Via J**: M is directly connected to J.

From this analysis, it's clear that all paths from B to M must pass through either E (which covers paths through E and L) or J.

Let's list some representative paths to confirm this:
*   **Paths through E (or L via E) to M**:
    *   B -> H -> E -> M
    *   B -> H -> E -> L -> M
*   **Paths through J to M**:
    *   B -> H -> I -> J -> M
    *   B -> H -> I -> P -> J -> M
    *   B -> C -> I -> J -> M
    *   B -> C -> I -> P -> J -> M
    *   B -> C -> O -> P -> J -> M
    *   B -> N -> C -> I -> J -> M
    *   B -> N -> C -> I -> P -> J -> M
    *   B -> N -> C -> O -> P -> J -> M
    *   B -> N -> O -> P -> J -> M

Now, I consider potential cut sets:

*   **Single node removal**:
    *   If I remove E: Paths through E and L are broken. However, paths through J (e.g., B -> H -> I -> J -> M) still exist. So, removing E alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove J: Paths through J are broken. However, paths through E (e.g., B -> H -> E -> M) still exist. So, removing J alone is not enough.
    *   Removing any other single node (H, C, N, I, O, P, L, etc.) would also leave at least one path intact. For example, removing H would leave paths like B -> C -> I -> J -> M.

*   **Two node removal**:
    *   Consider removing nodes E and J.
        *   Removing E breaks all paths that go through E directly to M (B -> ... -> E -> M).
        *   Removing E also breaks all paths that go through L to M, because L is only connected to E (other than M). So, B -> ... -> E -> L -> M paths are broken.
        *   Removing J breaks all paths that go through J directly to M (B -> ... -> J -> M).
    *   Since all paths from B to M must pass through either E (or L via E) or J, removing both E and J will sever all connections between B and M.

Therefore, the set {E, J} is a cut set of size 2. Since a single node removal was insufficient, 2 is the minimum number of nodes required.

    *   B - N - Y - O - P - J - I - H
    (All these paths eventually use the edge (I, H) to reach H)

    Since removing any two of these three edges leaves at least one path, a cut set of size 2 is not sufficient.

Since 1 or 2 edges are not sufficient, and a set of 3 edges (specifically, the edges directly connected to H) successfully disconnects B and H, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    *   E - B - C - N - Y - O - P - J

*   **Disjoint Path A:** E - M - J (Intermediate node: {M})
*   **Disjoint Path B:** E - F - X - G - H - I - J (Intermediate nodes: {F, X, G, H, I})

*   Set A: {M}
*   Set B: {F, X, G, H, I}
*   Set C: {B, C, O, P}

These three sets of intermediate nodes are completely disjoint. According to Menger's Theorem, if there are `k` vertex-disjoint paths between two nodes, then the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect them is at least `k`. In this case, `k=3`. For example, taking M from Path A, F from Path B, and B from Path C.

1.  **Remove M:** This breaks Path 1 (E-M-J) and Path 2 (E-L-M-J).

*   J (via edge (M, J))

2.  Remove (M, E)
3.  Remove (M, J)

After removing these three edges, node M has no remaining connections to any other node in the graph. Therefore, it is impossible for any path to exist from M to B. This constitutes a valid cut set. The size of this cut set is 3.

Now, I need to verify if a smaller cut set (e.g., 1 or 2 edges) could also disconnect M and B.

**Attempt to find a cut set of size 1:**
Removing any single edge will not disconnect M from B. For example, if (M, E) is removed, paths like M-L-E-B or M-J-P-O-N-B would still exist. If (E, B) is removed, paths like M-E-F-K-G-H-B or M-J-P-O-N-B would still exist. Thus, a single edge is insufficient.

**Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**
Let's consider various combinations of two edges:
*   **Removing two edges directly connected to M:**
    *   If (M, L) and (M, E) are removed, the path M-J-P-O-N-B still exists.
    *   If (M, L) and (M, J) are removed, the path M-E-B still exists.
    *   If (M, E) and (M, J) are removed, the path M-L-E-B still exists.
    Therefore, removing any two edges directly connected to M is not sufficient.
*   **Removing two edges directly connected to B:**
    *   Node B is connected to E, N, C, H. If we remove any two, for example (E, B) and (N, B), paths like M-E-F-K-G-H-B (via H) and M-J-P-I-C-B (via C) would still exist.
    Therefore, removing any two edges directly connected to B is not sufficient.
*   **Removing two "central" edges:**
    *   Consider paths that branch out from M: one branch goes through E (M-E-...), and another through J (M-J-...).
    *   Paths through E can go directly to B (M-E-B) or via F-K-G-H (M-E-F-K-G-H-B).
    *   Paths through J go via P, then O or I, then to N, C, or H, and finally to B.
    *   If we remove (E, B) and (P, O):
        *   Paths M-E-F-K-G-H-B (via H) still exist.
        *   Paths M-J-P-I-C-B (via I and C) still exist.
    *   If we remove (E, F) and (P, I):
        *   Paths M-E-B (direct via E) still exist.
        *   Paths M-J-P-O-N-B (via O and N) still exist.
    In all tested scenarios, removing two edges leaves at least one path between M and B.

Since a cut set of size 1 or 2 is not sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove must be 3. The cut set [(M, L), (M, E), (M, J)] successfully disconnects M from B.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from N.

1.  **Identify the degrees of the target nodes:**
    *   Node G is connected to E, F, H, and K. So, degree(G) = 4.
    *   Node N is connected to B, C, and Y. So, degree(N) = 3.

2.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   To isolate N, I can remove all edges connected to it: (N, B), (N, C), and (N, Y). This would be a cut of size 3.
    *   To isolate G, I can remove all edges connected to it: (G, E), (G, F), (G, H), and (G, K). This would be a cut of size 4.
    *   Since a cut of size 3 is possible by isolating N, the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 3.

3.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths (Menger's Theorem):**
    To confirm if 3 is indeed the minimum, I will try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between G and N. If I can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut must be at least 3.

    Let's identify three distinct paths from G to N that do not share any edges:

    *   **Path 1:** G - E - B - N
        Edges: (G, E), (E, B), (B, N)

    *   **Path 2:** G - H - I - C - N
        Edges: (G, H), (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)

    *   **Path 3:** G - F - E - L - M - J - P - O - Y - N
        Edges: (G, F), (F, E), (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, Y), (Y, N)

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness:**
    Let's check if any edges are shared between these three paths:
    *   Edges in Path 1: (G, E), (E, B), (B, N)
    *   Edges in Path 2: (G, H), (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   Edges in Path 3: (G, F), (F, E), (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, Y), (Y, N)

    Upon careful inspection, all edges listed for Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are unique and do not overlap. For example, (G,E) in Path 1 is distinct from (F,E) in Path 3.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and N, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect G and N is at least 3.
    As established in step 2, removing the three edges connected to N (N, B), (N, C), and (N, Y) successfully disconnects N from the rest of the graph, and thus from G. This is a cut of size 3.
    Since we found a cut of size 3 and proved that at least 3 edges are required, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(N, B), (N, C), (N, Y)]
