2.  **Paths starting with E -> M:**
    *   E - M - J - I
    *   E - M - J - P - I

3.  **Paths starting with E -> G:**
    *   E - G - H - I
    *   E - G - F - H - I
    *   E - G - K - F - H - I
    *   E - G - X - F - H - I

Upon reviewing all these paths, I observe a critical pattern:
*   All paths that start with E -> H (Path 1) pass through H.
*   All paths that start with E -> M (Paths 2, 3) pass through M.
*   All paths that start with E -> G (Paths 4, 5, 6, 7) eventually lead to H to reach I (e.g., E-G-H-I, E-G-F-H-I, etc.). There are no paths from G to I that do not involve H.

This means that every single path from E to I must pass through either node H or node M.

To disconnect E and I, I must break all these paths.
*   If I remove node H, all paths that go through H (Paths 1, 4, 5, 6, 7) will be broken.
*   The remaining paths are those that go through M (Paths 2, 3).
*   If I then remove node M, these remaining paths will also be broken.

Therefore, removing both H and M will ensure that no path exists between E and I. This gives a cut set of size 2: [H, M].

Now, I need to verify if a smaller cut set (i.e., a single node) is possible.
Let's check if removing any single node disconnects E and I:
*   Removing H: Paths E-M-J-I and E-M-J-P-I still exist. (Not enough)
*   Removing M: Paths E-H-I, E-G-H-I, E-G-F-H-I, E-G-K-F-H-I, E-G-X-F-H-I still exist. (Not enough)
*   Removing G: Paths E-H-I, E-M-J-I, E-M-J-P-I still exist. (Not enough)
*   Removing J: Paths E-H-I, E-G-H-I, E-G-F-H-I, E-G-K-F-H-I, E-G-X-F-H-I still exist. (Not enough)
*   Removing C or P: Paths E-H-I, E-M-J-I, E-M-J-P-I (if P is not removed), and all G-H paths still exist. (Not enough)
*   Removing F, K, or X: Paths E-H-I, E-M-J-I, E-M-J-P-I, and E-G-H-I still exist. (Not enough)
*   Removing any other node (L, D, N, O, Y, B): These nodes are not on all paths between E and I, and some are not on any path. (Not enough)

Since no single node can disconnect E and I, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2. The cut set [H, M] achieves disconnection with 2 nodes.

4.  **Verify Minimality using Menger's Theorem:** To confirm that 3 is the minimum number of edges, I need to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between H and N. If I can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum cut size is 3.
    Let's identify three distinct (edge-disjoint) paths from H to N:

    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G (black) and N (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from N.

First, I identify the direct neighbors of G and N:
- Neighbors of G: F, X, K, H
- Neighbors of N: B, C, Y, O

All paths from G to N must pass through one of G's neighbors, and eventually reach one of N's neighbors, and then use the edge to N.

Let's consider the edges directly connected to N: (N,B), (N,C), (N,Y), (N,O).
If we remove all four of these edges, N would be completely isolated, thus disconnected from G. This gives an upper bound of 4 edges.

Now, let's try to find a smaller cut set.
Consider the nodes B, C, O, Y as "gateways" to N. Any path from G to N must pass through one of these nodes and then use the direct edge to N.

Let's analyze the connections of Y and O:
- Y is connected to N and O.
- O is connected to N, C, P, Y.

If we remove the edge (N,O), then O is disconnected from N. Since Y's only other connection is to O, if O cannot reach N, then Y also cannot reach N from any path originating from G (as any path to Y must pass through O). Therefore, if (N,O) is removed, the edge (N,Y) becomes redundant for disconnecting G from N.

This suggests a potential cut set of 3 edges: [(N,B), (N,C), (N,O)].
Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects G from N:
1.  **Removing (N,B):** This breaks all paths that end with the edge (B,N). Examples: G-H-B-N, G-F-E-B-N, G-F-M-L-E-B-N.
2.  **Removing (N,C):** This breaks all paths that end with the edge (C,N). Examples: G-H-I-C-N.
3.  **Removing (N,O):** This breaks all paths that end with the edge (O,N). Examples: G-H-I-P-O-N, G-H-I-J-P-O-N, G-H-I-J-D-P-O-N. As explained above, since Y is only connected to N and O, and O is now disconnected from N, any path from G to Y (which must pass through O) cannot reach N. Thus, the path through Y is also effectively broken.

Since all paths from G to N must pass through B, C, O, or Y, and we have effectively cut off all these entry points to N, the set [(N,B), (N,C), (N,O)] successfully disconnects G from N. This is a cut of size 3.

Now, I need to prove that it's impossible to disconnect G and N with only 2 edges.
Let's consider various scenarios for removing 2 edges:

**Scenario 1: Removing two edges directly connected to N.**
*   If we remove (N,B) and (N,C): Paths like G-H-I-P-O-N still exist.
*   If we remove (N,B) and (N,O): Paths like G-H-I-C-N still exist.
*   If we remove (N,C) and (N,O): Paths like G-H-B-N (and G-F-E-B-N, G-F-M-L-E-B-N) still exist.
In all these cases, N remains connected to G.

**Scenario 2: Removing two edges not directly connected to N.**
All paths from G must pass through H or F (since X and K only connect to G and F).
*   **Paths from G via H:**
    *   G-H-B-N
    *   G-H-I-C-N
    *   G-H-I-P-O-N
    *   G-H-I-J-P-O-N
    *   G-H-I-J-D-P-O-N
*   **Paths from G via F:**
    *   G-F-E-B-N
    *   G-F-M-L-E-B-N
    *   (Note: Paths like G-F-E-B-H-I-C-N are also possible, but they eventually use the H-branch, so cutting the H-branch would cover them.)

Let's try to cut one edge from the "H-branch" and one from the "F-branch":
*   If we remove (H,B) and (F,E): Paths like G-H-I-C-N and G-H-I-P-O-N still exist.
*   If we remove (H,I) and (F,E): Paths like G-H-B-N still exist.
*   If we remove (H,B) and (F,M): Paths like G-H-I-C-N and G-F-E-B-N still exist.

It is clear that due to the multiple alternative paths and the interconnectedness of the graph, no two edges are sufficient to disconnect G from N. For example, even if we cut all paths originating from H (e.g., by removing (H,B) and (H,I)), paths originating from F (e.g., G-F-E-B-N) would still exist. Conversely, cutting all paths from F (e.g., by removing (F,E) and (F,M)) would leave paths from H (e.g., G-H-B-N) intact.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges required is 3.

    *   Paths ending at H via G: E - F - G - H (uses edge (G, H))
    *   Paths ending at H via I: E - M - J - I - H and E - L - M - J - I - H (both use edge (I, H))

4.  **Determine the minimum cut:**
    *   To disconnect E from H, we must first remove the direct edge (E, H).
    *   After removing (E, H), we need to ensure that no path can reach H through B, G, or I.
    *   If we do not remove (B, H), then Path 1 (E - B - H) remains.
    *   If we do not remove (G, H), then Path 2 (E - F - G - H) remains.
    *   If we do not remove (I, H), then Paths 3 and 4 (E - M - J - I - H and E - L - M - J - I - H) remain.

    Therefore, to completely disconnect E from H, we must remove the direct edge (E, H) AND the edges (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   The proposed cut set is [(E, H), (B, H), (G, H), (I, H)], which contains 4 edges.
    *   Could we achieve disconnection with fewer than 4 edges?
    *   We know (E, H) must be removed. This leaves 3 edges to cut the remaining paths.
    *   If we remove only two edges from the set {(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)}, one of these edges will remain. For example, if we remove (B, H) and (G, H), the edge (I, H) remains, and paths E - M - J - I - H and E - L - M - J - I - H would still connect E to H.
    *   This demonstrates that removing any two edges from {(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)} is insufficient. Thus, all three edges (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H) are necessary to cut all paths entering H from its neighbors.
    *   Therefore, the minimum number of edges required is 1 (for (E, H)) + 3 (for (B, H), (G, H), (I, H)) = 4 edges.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(E, H), (B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

    *   P4: H - I - C - B
    *   P5: H - I - P - O - C - B
    *   P6: H - I - P - O - N - B

    To determine the minimum number of edges required (by Menger's Theorem), I look for the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between H and B.
    *   Path 1: H - B (Edges: {(H, B)})
    *   Path 2: H - G - F - E - B (Edges: {(H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, B)})
    *   Path 3: H - I - C - B (Edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, B)})

    These three paths (P1, P2, P3) are edge-disjoint. No edge is shared between any two of them.
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and B is at least 3.

3.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**

    After removing these three edges, H has no remaining connections to the rest of the graph that could lead to B. Therefore, H is completely disconnected from B.

5.  **Conclusion:**

2.  **Path via F:** E - F - H (via edges (E, F) and (F, H))

All such paths must pass through the node J, as J is the only node connecting the {L, M} cluster to the {I, P, B, C, N, O} cluster which leads to H.
Examples of such paths:
*   E - M - J - I - H

*   E - M - J - P - O - C - B - H
*   E - M - J - P - O - N - B - H

To disconnect E and H, I need to ensure that all these paths are broken.

**Strategy for finding a minimal cut set:**

*   Remove (E, H) to break the direct path.

*   Remove (G, H) to break paths via G (e.g., E-G-H).

*   H is still connected to B, I.
*   F and G are no longer connected to H.

Therefore, `C1` (3 edges) is not sufficient to disconnect E and H. This implies the minimum number of edges must be at least 4.

**Attempt 2: Extend the cut set to include indirect paths.**
After removing `C1 = {(E, H), (F, H), (G, H)}`, the remaining paths all go from E (via L or M) to J, and then from J to I or P, and finally to H (via I or B).
Crucially, all these remaining paths must pass through the edge (M, J) to get from the E-side (L, M) to the J-side.
If I remove (M, J), then:
*   E is connected to L and M.
*   L is connected to M.
*   M is no longer connected to J.
*   Since J is the only gateway from M (and L via M) to the rest of the graph that leads to H (i.e., I, P, B, C, N, O), removing (M, J) effectively disconnects E from all these indirect paths.

So, adding (M, J) to `C1` creates a new cut set:
`C2 = {(E, H), (F, H), (G, H), (M, J)}`. This set has 4 edges.

**Verification of C2:**
1.  **(E, H)**: Breaks the direct path E-H.
2.  **(F, H)**: Breaks paths like E-F-H.
3.  **(G, H)**: Breaks paths like E-G-H.
4.  **(M, J)**: Breaks all paths from E (via L or M) to J, and consequently to I, P, B, C, N, O, which are the only remaining ways to reach H.

With these 4 edges removed, no path exists between E and H.

**Checking for a smaller cut (size 3):**
I have already shown that `C1` (3 edges) is not enough. Let's try other combinations of 3 edges.
*   If I remove any 3 edges from the set `{(E, H), (F, H), (G, H), (M, J)}`, at least one of the four "path bundles" will remain connected. For example, removing (E, H), (F, H), (G, H) leaves the paths through (M, J) intact. Removing (E, H), (F, H), (M, J) leaves E-G-H intact.
*   Consider cutting edges connected to H: (H, E), (H, F), (H, G), (H, B), (H, I). If I pick any 3 of these, say (H, E), (H, F), (H, G), then paths like E-M-J-I-H or E-M-J-P-O-C-B-H would still exist.
*   Consider cutting edges connected to E: (E, H), (E, F), (E, G), (E, L), (E, M). If I pick any 3 of these, say (E, H), (E, F), (E, G), then paths like E-L-M-J-I-H would still exist.

Based on this systematic exploration, a cut set of 3 edges is not sufficient. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(E, H), (F, H), (G, H), (M, J)]

    *   **Path 1 (P1):** G - H - B - N
        Edges in P1: {(G, H), (H, B), (B, N)}

    *   **Path 2 (P2):** G - E - M - J - P - O - N
        Edges in P2: {(G, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)}

        Edges in P3: {(G, F), (F, E), (E, H), (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)}

3.  **Verify Edge-Disjointness:**
    *   Let's list all edges used across these three paths and check for any overlaps:
        *   Edges from P1: (G, H), (H, B), (B, N)
        *   Edges from P2: (G, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)
        *   Edges from P3: (G, F), (F, E), (E, H), (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   Upon careful inspection, there are no common edges between any two of these three paths. For example, (G, H) is only in P1, (G, E) is only in P2, and (G, F) is only in P3. Similarly, (E, H) is only in P3, and it's not (G, H) or (G, E). All listed edges are unique to their respective paths.
    *   Therefore, these three paths (P1, P2, P3) are indeed edge-disjoint.

4.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size (Menger's Theorem):**
    *   According to Menger's Theorem, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect those two nodes.
    *   Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

5.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:**

    *   The relevant edges are:
        *   (G, E)
        *   (G, H)
        *   (F, E)
    *   Let's propose the cut set: `C_set = [(G, E), (G, H), (F, E)]`. This set contains 3 edges.

6.  **Verify the Cut Set:**
    *   If the edges (G, E), (G, H), and (F, E) are removed from the graph:
        *   Node G is now only connected to X, K, and F.
        *   Node F is now only connected to G, X, and K (its connection to E is removed).
        *   Nodes X and K are only connected to G and F.

    *   Since N is not part of the {G, X, K, F} subgraph, G is effectively disconnected from N.
    *   Therefore, `[(G, E), (G, H), (F, E)]` is a valid cut set.

7.  **Conclusion:**

    *   We found a specific set of 3 edges whose removal successfully disconnects G and N.
    *   Thus, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node G (black): Connected to E, F, H, B.
    *   Node I (black): Connected to H, J, C, O, P.

2.  **Find edge-disjoint paths between G and I to establish a lower bound for the cut size.**
    I will look for paths that do not share any common edges.
    *   **Path 1:** G - H - I
        Edges: (G,H), (H,I)
    *   **Path 2:** G - B - C - I
        Edges: (G,B), (B,C), (C,I)
    *   **Path 3:** G - E - M - J - I
        Edges: (G,E), (E,M), (M,J), (J,I)

    These three paths (P1, P2, P3) are edge-disjoint. This means that any set of edges that disconnects G from I must contain at least one edge from each of these three paths. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

3.  **Explore potential cut sets to find an upper bound.**
    *   **Option A: Isolate G.**
        To completely disconnect G from the rest of the graph, I can remove all edges connected to G.
        Edges connected to G: (G,E), (G,F), (G,H), (G,B).
        Removing these 4 edges would isolate G, ensuring no path to I. This is a valid cut set of size 4.

    *   **Option B: Isolate I.**
        To completely disconnect I from the rest of the graph, I can remove all edges connected to I.
        Edges connected to I: (I,H), (I,J), (I,C), (I,O), (I,P).
        Removing these 5 edges would isolate I, ensuring no path to G. This is a valid cut set of size 5.

    Since I found a cut set of size 4 (Option A), the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 4.

4.  **Determine if a cut set of size 3 exists.**
    Since the lower bound is 3 and the upper bound is 4, the minimum cut size is either 3 or 4. I need to rigorously check if a cut of 3 edges is possible.

    Let's try to find a set of 3 edges that would disconnect G and I.
    Consider the "entry points" to I from the perspective of G: H, C, O, J, P.
    Let's try removing 3 edges that connect to I:
    *   **Attempt 1: Remove [(I,H), (I,C), (I,J)]**
        *   Paths through H (e.g., G-H-I) are broken.
        *   Paths through C (e.g., G-B-C-I) are broken.
        *   Paths through J (e.g., G-E-M-J-I) are broken.

        *   Path G - B - C - O - I (uses (O,I))
        *   Path G - E - M - J - P - I (uses (P,I))
        Therefore, this set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

        *   Path G - B - H - I (uses (B,H) and (H,I), which are not cut)
        *   Path G - B - N - Y - O - I (uses (B,N), (N,Y), (Y,O), (O,I), which are not cut)
        Therefore, this set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

        *   (C,I) breaks G-B-C-I, G-E-B-C-I.

        Therefore, this set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

    *   (O,I) breaks all paths G-...-O-I.

If we remove these three edges, H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from B. This gives us a potential cut set of size 3: `[(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]`.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut set exists (e.g., 1 or 2 edges):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that it's impossible to disconnect B and H by removing only 1 or 2 edges. This can be done by finding edge-disjoint paths between B and H. If there are `k` edge-disjoint paths, then at least `k` edges must be removed to disconnect the nodes (Menger's Theorem).

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from B to H:
    *   **Path 1:** B - H
        *   Edges: `{(B, H)}`

    *   **Path 2:** B - C - I - H
        *   Edges: `{(B, C), (C, I), (I, H)}`

        *   Edges: `{(B, E), (E, F), (F, K), (K, G), (G, H)}`
        *   This path does not share any edges with Path 1 or Path 2.

    I have identified three distinct paths between B and H that do not share any common edges.
    *   Path 1 uses edge (B, H).
    *   Path 2 uses edges (B, C), (C, I), (I, H).
    *   Path 3 uses edges (B, E), (E, F), (F, K), (K, G), (G, H).

    Since these three paths are edge-disjoint, any set of edges that disconnects B from H must contain at least one edge from each of these three paths. Therefore, a minimum of 3 edges must be removed.

4.  **Confirm the minimal cut set:**
    The cut set identified in step 2, `[(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]`, has a size of 3.
    *   Removing (B, H) breaks Path 1.
    *   Removing (I, H) breaks Path 2 (and any other path ending at I-H).
    *   Removing (G, H) breaks Path 3 (and any other path ending at G-H).
    Since these three edges are sufficient to break all paths (as any path to H must use one of these three edges as its final step), and we've established that at least 3 edges are required, this is indeed the minimum cut set.
